    "evidence_text": "Self-healing materials that autonomously and repeatedly\nrepair physical damage through dynamic physical or che-\nmical bonds1–7have gained signi ﬁcant levels of attention in\na variety of material ﬁelds, including automobile components8,9,\nelectronics10–14, robotics15,16, and healthcare devices17–20. How-\never, the trade-off between mechanical and healing performance\nis the biggest hurdle that these materials face and need to over-\ncome. The high chain rigidity, entanglement, and crystallinity\nrequired for mechanical strength con ﬂict with the high diffusi-\nbility and exchange of dynamic bonds required for repairing\ndamage21–24. Consequently, materials that autonomously heal at\nambient temperature exhibit mechanical performance that is\nunsatisfactory for industrial commercialization.\nIn contrast, biological tissue is not only able to ef ﬁciently self-\nrepair but is also very tough and resilient25,26. The secrets of some\nbiological mechano-responsive processes that toughen bioma-\nterials have recently been unveiled, but such insight remains\nundiscovered or undeveloped for man-made self-healing\nmaterials27,28. For example, the egg-capsule wall of a channeled\nwhelk effectively absorbs shock with a high degree of reversible\nextensibility and stiffness29; its constituent proteins undergo\nreversible structural α-helix to β-sheet transitions with internal-\nenergy changes when loaded. After the removal of stress, energy\nrelaxation results in the recovery of a high degree of strain. As\nanother example, the toughening and shape memory of spider\nsilk is the result of the arrangement and density of the inter-\nmolecular hydrogen (H)-bonds in silk crystals that are tuned by\nthe spinning rate from the spider ’s secretion gland30–32.\nHerein, we report a self-healing carbonate-type thermoplastic\npolyurethane (TPU) elastomer with remarkable ultimate tensile\nstrength (UTS) of 43 MPa for a self-healing elastomer that\nfunctions at ambient temperature; this TPU even outperforms a\ncommercial TPU used as a footwear component33–38. The rich\ncarbonyl groups of the carbonate soft segments form H-bonds. As\nopposed to a typical TPU, its hard segments are poorly stacked,\nthereby providing a fully amorphous phase as well as poor phase\nseparation. In addition, this TPU exhibits favorable biocompat-ibility. Thus, this material holds great potential as a structural\nmaterial for electronic skin (e-skin) and soft robotics in health-\ncare systems.\nMechano-responsive H-bond biomimicry involving the unique\ncharacteristics of the respective hard and softs segments is the key\nto the great performance of this material. The H-bonding array of\ncarbonyl groups undergoes a reversible structural change upon\nexternal stress. In static mode, the amorphous phase enables the\nsegmental swapping of H-bonds and the dynamic formation of\naromatic disul ﬁdes, which enables self-repair in the absence of\nstress, with molecular ﬂow retarded due to the abundant carbonyl\nH-bonds that increase the elastic modulus. Under strain, the\namorphous phase is transformed into a rigid metastable crystal\nwhere ordered H-bonds form between stretched chains to endure\nheavy loads. After removal of the stress, the metastable crystal\nreturns to the amorphous phase, with its shape and strain fully\nrecovered, and this elastomer is immediately available for self-\nhealing.\nResults\nPreparing a strong self-healing elastomer that functions at\nambient temperature . The self-healing TPU referred to as C-IP-\nSS was synthesized from poly(hexamethylene carbonate) diol (C,\na carbonate-type aliphatic macrodiol) as the soft segment,\nasymmetric alicyclic isophorone diisocyanate (IP) as the hard\nsegment, and an aromatic disul ﬁde (SS) as the chain extender\n(Fig. 1a and Supplementary Fig. 1)\n39–44. SS metathesis drives\nintrinsic self ‐healing at room temperature45,46. Our previousself-healing TPU (referred to as E-IP-SS), which contains ether-\ntype macrodiol (E), IP, and SS units47, was used as the control.\nThe number-average molecular weights (M ns) of the carbonate-\nand ether-type macrodiols are 1 kg mol−1and their compositions\nin each self-healing TPU are almost equal (15 wt%) (Supple-\nmentary Figs. 2-6 and Supplementary Table 1). Commercial TPU\n(Es-MD), containing ester-type macrodiol (Es) and methylene\ndiphenyl diisocyanate (MD) units, was used as a non-self-healable\ncontrol. The weight-average molecular weights (M ws) of C-IP-SS,\nE-IP-SS, and Es-MD were 24, 46, and 99 kg mol−1, respectively.\nInterestingly, C-IP-SS exhibited signi ﬁcantly superior mechan-\nical properties compared to other self-healing materials. The\ntensile properties of the three types of TPU ﬁlm prepared by\nsolvent casting were examined. Prior to this study, E-IP-SS\nexhibited the highest toughness (26.9 MJ m−3) and UTS (6.8\nMPa) among self-healing materials that function at ambient\ntemperature (Fig. 1b). Nevertheless, its tensile performance is\ntrivial compared to that of Es-MD, with toughness and UTS of\n115 MJ m−3and 36 MPa, respectively. Although C-IP-SS has the\nlowest M wamong the TPUs, its UTS of 43 MPa is higher than the\nother two control TPUs. It exhibits two-fold lower extensibility\n(450%) but a 2.8-fold greater toughness (75 MJ m−3) than E-IP-\nSS (Fig. 1a, c).\nThe outstanding tensile properties of C-IP-SS have little\npenalty on its self-healing ef ﬁciency. C-IP-SS ﬁlms were cut in\nhalf, reattached, and then allowed to recover at 35 °C for varying\namounts of time (Fig. 1b, c and Supplementary Table 2). UTS\nrecoveries of 29%, 39%, 65%, and 77% were observed after 1, 6,\n24, and 48 h, respectively. Notably, the UTS of the recovered C-\nIP-SS exceeded that of virgin E-IP-SS after only 1 h of self-\nhealing; after 48 h, the recovered C-IP-SS is as strong as Es-MD\n(Fig. 1c, d and Supplementary Movie 1), and another cut and re-\nspliced ﬁlm with a contact cut-area of 30 mm × 3 mm was able to\nreadily lift a 10-kg weight (Fig. 1e and Supplementary Movie 2).\nEven after 1 min of recovery at room temperature (25 °C), a cut\nand re-spliced ﬁlm with a contacted cut-area of 5 mm × 1 mm\nwas able to withstand manual drawing and twisting (Fig. 1f and\nSupplementary Movie 3). According to the Ashby plot of UTS\nversus self-healing temperature for recently reported elastomers,\nprevious tensile strengths did not exceed 20 MPa without the aid\nof light or a temperature above 40 °C (Fig. 2and Supplementary\nTable 3)9,21–24,34–40,42,43,47–51.\nAchieving both self-healing and strength through reversible\nstructural change . The breakthrough in the trade-off between\nself-healing and strength achieved for C-IP-SS is attributable to a\ndual mode of operation involving its mechano-responsive H-\nbonding array, with reversible strain-induced crystallization\nproviding a major contribution. In the stress –strain curve for C-\nIP-SS (Fig. 1b), the concave upward trend is a clear sign of strain\nhardening. Figure 3a reveals that the tensile moduli (i.e., differ-\nential stress values) increase sharply with strain and are sub-\nstantially different from those of E-IP-SS and Es-MD. C-IP-SS\nexhibits a more than 4.3-fold higher modulus than the other\nTPUs at 400% elongation, and becomes translucent due to the\nformation of crystallites, while the other TPUs remain relatively\ntransparent (Fig. 3b, c and Supplementary Figs. 7 and 8). Inter-\nestingly, the original strain and transparency of C-IP-SS are fully\nrecovered upon unloading (Supplementary Fig. 9 and Supple-\nmentary Movie 4); strain-induced crystallization upon loading is\nsuper ﬁcially incompatible with full recovery upon unloading; it\ncannot be explained either by the solid crystallization of semi-\ncrystalline polymers or entropic elastomer elasticity because the\nformer does not facilitate strain recovery, while the latter does\nnot enable phase transition.     "evidence_text": "5,545,708 \n 5 \nthane which utilizes only a single chain extender, the HS tends to be highly crystalline and rigid. In the present \ninvention, the side chain branching of the second chain extender is believed to introduce mobility, hence flexibility, into thc HS by disrupting the linearity and the packing in the \nHS and reduces the differentiation between the HS and SS. \nThis reduced differentiation between the HS and SS leads to \na reduction in the initial bend force or stiffness for the \npresent polyurethane when compared to many commercial polyurethanes at comparable HS. The present polyurethane is preferably prepared from a \nmixture of diol chain extenders. The following examples are \nprovided to further illustrate the invention but are not to be \nconsidered as limitative of the invention. \n EXAMPLE I \nGeneral procedure for synthesis \nIn a well stirred reactor vessel, PTMEG-1000, MDI and \nchain extender were combined with effective mixing at room temperature. As used herein, the term \"effective mixing” describes thoroughly mixing the components without sub \nstantial aeration. An exotherm to about 100° C. develops and stirring is continued for up to about three minutes. The resulting viscous mixture is then maintained at about 125°C. for about one hour to ensure a substantially complete cure. The resultant polyurethane is then allowed to cool forming \na rigid resin which is then chipped to a size suitable for melt forming. \nTable I displays the components of a series of polyure \nthanes of the present invention prepared according to the procedure of Example I. However, PU-HM\nexhibits the smallest contact angle of 72.3◦among these five PUs, indicating the highest\nhydrophilicity. This may be attributed to the minimal proportion of ordered hydrogen\nbonds in PU-HM, allowing for interactions between disordered hydrogen bonds and free\npolar groups with water molecules. Additionally, due to the larger molecular volume of\nHMDI, the molecular stacking of PU-HM is relatively loose, resulting in higher surface\nroughness of the final product, thus enhancing the material’s hydrophilicity.\nFigure 5d shows the stress–strain curves of the PUs. Combining Figure 5d and Table S4 reveals that\nPU-M, with the highest degree of hydrogen bonding and superior crystallization, as well\nas rigid phenyl rings, exhibits the highest tensile strength of 23.4 MPa. PU-H, possessing the lowest hydrogen bonding but\nexcellent crystallization, displays the highest modulus and hardness. Additionally, the\ncrystalline regions act as physical crosslinking points, enhancing strength to some extent,\nwith a tensile strength of 10.8 MPa.     "evidence_text": "Polymers 2024 ,16, 3045 9 of 14\nindicates that when PUs have a higher degree of hydrogen bonding, polar groups tend\nto form hydrogen bonds internally, reducing surface energy and decreasing interaction\nwith external water molecules, resulting in reduced hydrophilicity. However, PU-HM\nexhibits the smallest contact angle of 72.3◦among these five PUs, indicating the highest\nhydrophilicity. This may be attributed to the minimal proportion of ordered hydrogen\nbonds in PU-HM, allowing for interactions between disordered hydrogen bonds and free\npolar groups with water molecules. Additionally, due to the larger molecular volume of\nHMDI, the molecular stacking of PU-HM is relatively loose, resulting in higher surface\nroughness of the final product, thus enhancing the material’s hydrophilicity.\nFigure 5d shows the stress–strain curves of the PUs. The abundant hydrogen bonding\nwithin the HS promotes aggregation and crystallization, serving as the primary sources of\nstrength via physical crosslinking points. Combining Figure 5d and Table S4 reveals that\nPU-M, with the highest degree of hydrogen bonding and superior crystallization, as well\nas rigid phenyl rings, exhibits the highest tensile strength of 23.4 MPa. PU-HM, structurally\nsimilar to PU-M but with slightly lower hydrogen bonding, also demonstrates relatively\nhigh tensile strength at 18.1 MPa. PU-H, possessing the lowest hydrogen bonding but\nexcellent crystallization, displays the highest modulus and hardness. Additionally, the\ncrystalline regions act as physical crosslinking points, enhancing strength to some extent,\nwith a tensile strength of 10.8 MPa. However, extensive crystallization reduces material\ntoughness, resulting in an elongation at break of only 144%. PU-T, due to its poor molecular\nsymmetry, lower HS aggregation, and hydrogen bonding, exhibits a modest tensile strength\nof 3.2 MPa but boasts a remarkable elongation at break of 779%, the highest among the five\nPUs. During the experiment, PU-H and PU-M did not exhibit any significant mechanical\nproperty restoration, possibly due to their higher crystallinity and molecular rigidity, which\nhindered the diffusion and interaction of molecular chains between the fracture surfaces\nat 80◦C. PU-IP and PU-T demonstrated high healing efficiencies of 88.7% and 87.5%,\nrespectively, whereas PU-HM exhibited a healing efficiency of 51.9%. This suggests that\nhigh crystallinity and molecular chain rigidity are detrimental to self-healing performance,\nwhile relatively lower crystallinity and loosely packed molecular chains are more conducive\nto the diffusion of molecular chains and the formation of interactions such as hydrogen\nbonding, resulting in improved healing effects.\n3.4.     "evidence_text": "Appl. Sci. 2021 ,11, 5359 12 of 17\nstructure to break up at temperatures between 50 and 75◦C, resulting in the intermolecular\nforces collapsing and the storage modulus signiﬁcantly decreasing. This result is presented\nin Figure 8b, indicating that the Tan δof TPU-PTC-BDO increased dramatically between 50\nand 75◦C. In a glassy state, the TPU-PTC copolymers possess more carbonate groups so\nthat the polymer chains are restricted in mobility, contrary to the TPU-PHC copolymers\nthat own the long carbon chains [50].\n3.6. Tensile Strength\nThe molecular weight of a polymer is a key factor affecting its tensile strength. As\nmentioned in Section 3.3, the TPU copolymers using the same chain extender had com-\nparable molecular weights. Thus, the molecular weight can be ignored in the analysis\nof tensile strength. As Table 6 indicates, the TPU had high elongations at break (above\n460%), and both the TPU-PHC-PDO and TPU-PHC-BDO had moderate strain at break.\nHowever, the TPU-PHC-BDO and TPU-PTC-PDO differed considerably in terms of the\nstrain at break. The results are possibly affected by hydrogen bonding. According to\nthe FT-IR results in Section 3.1 (Scheme 1a), the TPU-PHC-BDO and TPU-PHC-PDO had\nstrong intermolecular hydrogen bonds in the TPU-PTC-BDO; because the regularity of the\nmolecular chains was poor (atactic) and the intermolecular attraction weak, poor strength\nresulted. Notably, TPU-PTC-PDO uses an odd-numbered polyol and chain extender to\ncause regular molecular chains (isotactic), stacking well, and more secondary bonds (such\nas hydrogen bonds, benzene π−πstacking, and dipole–dipole interactions) (Scheme 1b).\nBoth of the copolymers had the same number of the hydrogen bonds per unit in Scheme\n1b, and whereas we can see that Scheme 1b focuses on the blue and red squares in the\nsoft segments, TPU-PTC-PDO could have possible dipole–dipole force ( δ+toδ−) and\nTPU-PTC-BDO does not ( δ−toδ−). More dipole interactions for TPU copolymers can drive\nbetter mechanical strength. According to the literature, the storage modulus increases with\ndipole interactions under a glassy state [ 51]. We also saw this phenomenon in this study,\nand Figure 8a in the manuscript revealed that the storage modulus of TPU-PTC-PDO was\nhigher than TPU-PTC-BDO., It can be compared to Scheme 1b to prove that TPU-PTC-PDO\nhas more dipole interactions than TPU-PTC-BDO. Thus, it has a high tensile strength\nclose to TPU-PHC-BDO and TPU-PHC-PDO (Table 6). The regularity of molecular chains\nalso affected the stretching process. As is shown in Figure 9a,b, the stress of the TPU\nsamples presented a rapid rise at approximately 300% of elongation, except in the case of\nTPU-PTC-BDO. This was because TPU-PTC-BDO is an odd-numbered carbon chain (a long\nchain of three carbons) corresponding to an even-numbered carbon chain (a short chain\nof four carbons), and it cannot be arranged regularly after stress as is expected in strain-\ninduced crystallization [ 52–55]. Therefore, the intermolecular hydrogen bonding effect for\nthe TPU-PTC-BDO was lower than for other samples. Accordingly, the TPU-PTC-BDO\npresented the lowest stress but the highest elongation.\nTable 6.     "evidence_text": "5,545,708 \n 5 \nthane which utilizes only a single chain extender, the HS tends to be highly crystalline and rigid. In the present \ninvention, the side chain branching of the second chain extender is believed to introduce mobility, hence flexibility, into thc HS by disrupting the linearity and the packing in the \nHS and reduces the differentiation between the HS and SS. \nThis reduced differentiation between the HS and SS leads to \na reduction in the initial bend force or stiffness for the \npresent polyurethane when compared to many commercial polyurethanes at comparable HS. The present polyurethane is preferably prepared from a \nmixture of diol chain extenders. The following examples are \nprovided to further illustrate the invention but are not to be \nconsidered as limitative of the invention. \n EXAMPLE I \nGeneral procedure for synthesis \nIn a well stirred reactor vessel, PTMEG-1000, MDI and \nchain extender were combined with effective mixing at room temperature. As used herein, the term \"effective mixing” describes thoroughly mixing the components without sub \nstantial aeration. An exotherm to about 100° C. develops and stirring is continued for up to about three minutes. The resulting viscous mixture is then maintained at about 125°C. for about one hour to ensure a substantially complete cure. The resultant polyurethane is then allowed to cool forming \na rigid resin which is then chipped to a size suitable for melt forming. \nTable I displays the components of a series of polyure \nthanes of the present invention prepared according to the procedure of Example I.
